class="interlanguage-link interwiki-sk mw-list-item"><a href="https://sk.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkuin" title="Alkuin – Slovak" lang="sk" hreflang="sk" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Slovenčina</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-sl mw-list-item"><a href="https://sl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkuin" title="Alkuin – Slovenian" lang="sl" hreflang="sl" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Slovenščina</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-sr mw-list-item"><a href="https://sr.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%90%D0%BB%D0%BA%D1%83%D0%B8%D0%BD" title="Алкуин – Serbian" lang="sr" hreflang="sr" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Српски / srpski</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-sh mw-list-item"><a href="https://sh.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkuin" title="Alkuin – Serbo-Croatian" lang="sh" hreflang="sh" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Srpskohrvatski / српскохрватски</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-fi mw-list-item"><a href="https://fi.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkuin" title="Alkuin – Finnish" lang="fi" hreflang="fi" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Suomi</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-sv mw-list-item"><a href="https://sv.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkuin" title="Alkuin – Swedish" lang="sv" hreflang="sv" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Svenska</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-tl mw-list-item"><a href="https://tl.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alcuino_ng_York" title="Alcuino ng York – Tagalog" lang="tl" hreflang="tl" class="interlanguage-link-target"><span>Tagalog</span></a></li><li class="interlanguage-link interwiki-uk mw-list-item"><a href="https://uk.wikipedia.org/wiki/%D0%90%D0%BB%D0%BA%D1%83%D1%97%D0%BD" title="Алкуїн – Ukrainian" lang="uk" hreflang="uk" 			<div class="after-portlet after-portlet-lang"><span class="wb-langlinks-edit wb-langlinks-link"><a href="https://www.wikidata.org/wiki/Special:EntityPage/Q154332#sitelinks-wikipedia" title="Edit interlanguage links" class="wbc-editpage">Edit links</a></span></div>

						<div id="siteSub" class="noprint">From Wikipedia, the free encyclopedia</div>

					<div id="contentSub"><div id="mw-content-subtitle"></div></div>
					
					
					<div id="mw-content-text" class="mw-body-content mw-content-ltr" lang="en" dir="ltr"><div class="mw-parser-output"><div class="shortdescription nomobile noexcerpt noprint searchaux" style="display:none">8th-century Northumbrian scholar, clergyman, poet, and teacher</div>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1033289096">.mw-parser-output .hatnote{font-style:italic}.mw-parser-output div.hatnote{padding-left:1.6em;margin-bottom:0.5em}.mw-parser-output .hatnote i{font-style:normal}.mw-parser-output .hatnote+link+.hatnote{margin-top:-0.5em}</style><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">This article is about the scholar Alcuin of York. For other uses, see <a href="/wiki/Alcuin_(disambiguation)" class="mw-disambig" title="Alcuin (disambiguation)">Alcuin (disambiguation)</a>.</div>
<p class="mw-empty-elt">

</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1066479718">.mw-parser-output .infobox-subbox{padding:0;border:none;margin:-3px;width:auto;min-width:100%;font-size:100%;clear:none;float:none;background-color:transparent}.mw-parser-output .infobox-3cols-child{margin:auto}.mw-parser-output .infobox .navbar{font-size:100%}body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-header,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-subheader,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-above,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-title,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-image,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-full-data,body.skin-minerva .mw-parser-output .infobox-below{text-align:center}</style><table class="infobox biography vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:125%;"><div class="fn" style="display:inline">Alcuin of York</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Raban-Maur Alcuin Otgar.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg/220px-Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="234" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg/330px-Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg 1.5x, class="infobox-caption">A Carolingian manuscript, c. 831. <a href="/wiki/Rabanus_Maurus" title="Rabanus Maurus">Rabanus Maurus</a> (left), with Alcuin (middle), dedicating his work to Archbishop <a title="Northumbria">Northumbria</a></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Died</th><td class="infobox-data">19 May 804 (aged around 69)<br /><div style="display:inline" class="deathplace"><a href="/wiki/Tours" title="Tours">Tours</a>, <a href="/wiki/Francia" title="Francia">Francia</a></div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Occupation</th><td class="infobox-data role"><a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">Deacon</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a></td></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-full-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header">Academic background</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Influences</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Ecgbert_of_York" title="Ecgbert of York">Ecgbert of York</a></td><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header">Academic work</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Era</th><td class="infobox-data"><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1126788409">.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul{line-height:inherit;list-style:none;margin:0;padding:0}.mw-parser-output .plainlist ol li,.mw-parser-output .plainlist ul li{margin-bottom:0}</style><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Medieval_philosophy" title="Medieval philosophy">Medieval philosophy</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance" title="Carolingian Renaissance">Carolingian Renaissance</a></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Main interests</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"/><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Mathematics" title="Mathematics">Mathematics</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">Philosophy</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Christian_theology" title="Christian theology">Christian theology</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Poetry" title="Poetry">Poetry</a></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Notable works</th><td class="infobox-data"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1126788409"/><div class="plainlist">
<ul><li><i><a href="/wiki/Propositiones_ad_Acuendos_Juvenes" title="Propositiones ad Acuendos Juvenes">Propositiones ad Acuendos Juvenes</a></i></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Quaestiones_in_Genesim" title="Quaestiones in Genesim">Quaestiones in Genesim</a></i></li></ul>
</div></td></tr><tr style="display:none"><td colspan="2">
</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p><b>Alcuin of York</b> (<span class="rt-commentedText nowrap"><span class="IPA nopopups noexcerpt" lang="en-fonipa"><a href="/wiki/Help:IPA/English" title="Help:IPA/English">/<span style="border-bottom:1px dotted"><span title="/ˈ/: primary stress follows">ˈ</span><span title="/æ/: &#39;a&#39; in &#39;bad&#39;">æ</span><span title="&#39;l&#39; in &#39;lie&#39;">l</span><span title="&#39;k&#39; in &#39;kind&#39;">k</span><span title="&#39;w&#39; in &#39;wind&#39;">w</span><span title="/ɪ/: &#39;i&#39; in &#39;kit&#39;">ɪ</span><span title="&#39;n&#39; in &#39;nigh&#39;">n</span></span>/</a></span></span>;<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">Flaccus Albinus Alcuinus</i>; <abbr title="circa">c.</abbr> 735 – 19 May 804)&#160;– also called <b>Ealhwine</b>, <b>Alhwin</b>, or <b>Alchoin</b>&#160;– was a scholar, clergyman, poet, and teacher from <a href="/wiki/York" title="York">York</a>, <a href="/wiki/Northumbria" title="Northumbria">Northumbria</a>. He was born around 735 and became the student of <a href="/wiki/Ecgbert_of_York" title="Ecgbert of York">Archbishop Ecgbert</a> at York. At the invitation of <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a>, he became a leading scholar and teacher at the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_dynasty" title="Carolingian dynasty">Carolingian court</a>, where he remained a figure in the 780s and 790s. Before that, he was also a court chancellor in Aachen.  "The most learned man anywhere to be found", according to <a href="/wiki/Einhard" title="Einhard">Einhard</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Vita_Karoli_Magni" title="Vita Karoli Magni">Life of Charlemagne</a></i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEEinhard196054_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEEinhard196054-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> (<abbr title="circa">c.</abbr><span style="white-space:nowrap;">&#8201;817</span>–833), he is considered among the most important intellectual architects of the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance" title="Carolingian Renaissance">Carolingian Renaissance</a>. Among his pupils were many of the dominant intellectuals of the Carolingian era.
</p><p>During this  period, he perfected <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_minuscule" title="Carolingian minuscule">Carolingian minuscule</a>, an easily read manuscript hand using a mixture of upper- and lower-case letters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEColish199967_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEColish199967-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> Latin <a href="/wiki/Paleography" class="mw-redirect" title="Paleography">paleography</a> in the eighth century leaves little room for a single origin of the script, and sources contradict his importance as  no proof has been found of his direct involvement in the creation of the script.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup> Carolingian minuscule was already in use before Alcuin arrived in Francia.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup> Most likely he was responsible for copying and preserving the script<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> while at the same time restoring the purity of the form.<sup id="cite_ref-7" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alcuin wrote many theological and dogmatic treatises, as well as a few grammatical works and a number of poems. In 796, he was made abbot of <a href="/wiki/Marmoutier_Abbey,_Tours" title="Marmoutier Abbey, Tours">Marmoutier Abbey</a>, in <a href="/wiki/Tours" title="Tours">Tours</a>, where he remained until his death.
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biography">Biography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Background">Background</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Background">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:172px;"><a href="/wiki/File:AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum,_Vienna-8.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg/170px-AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="311" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg/255px-AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9e/AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg/340px-AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum%2C_Vienna-8.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2092" data-file-height="3826" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:AT_13763_Roof_figures_at_the_Kunsthistorisches_Museum,_Vienna-8.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Alcuin, roof figure, Museum of History of Arts, Vienna.</div></div></div>
<p>Alcuin was born in <a href="/wiki/Northumbria" title="Northumbria">Northumbria</a>, presumably sometime in the 730s. Virtually nothing is known of his parents, family background, or origin.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBullough2004164_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBullough2004164-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> In common hagiographical fashion, the <i>Vita Alcuini</i> asserts that Alcuin was "of noble English stock", and this statement has usually been accepted by scholars. Alcuin's own work only mentions such collateral kinsmen as Wilgils, father of the missionary saint <a href="/wiki/Willibrord" title="Willibrord">Willibrord</a>; and <a href="/wiki/Beornrad_(archbishop_of_Sens)" title="Beornrad (archbishop of Sens)">Beornrad</a> (also spelled Beornred), abbot of Echternach and bishop of Sens.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBullough2004146–47,_165_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBullough2004146–47,_165-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Willibrord, Alcuin and Beornrad were all related by blood.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMayr-Harting2016212_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMayr-Harting2016212-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStenton2001219_11-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStenton2001219-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his <i>Life</i> of St Willibrord, Alcuin writes that Wilgils, called a <i>paterfamilias</i>, had founded an oratory and church at the mouth of the <a href="/wiki/Humber" title="Humber">Humber</a>, which had fallen into Alcuin's possession by inheritance. Because in early Anglo-Latin writing <i>paterfamilias</i> ("head of a family, householder") usually referred to a <span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang"><a href="/wiki/Churl" title="Churl">ceorl</a></i></span> ("churl"), <a href="/wiki/Donald_A._Bullough" title="Donald A. Bullough">Donald A. Bullough</a> suggests that Alcuin's family was of <span title="Old English (ca. 450-1100)-language text"><i lang="ang">cierlisc</i></span> ("churlish") status: i.e., free but subordinate to a noble lord, and that Alcuin and other members of his family rose to prominence through beneficial connections with the aristocracy.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBullough2004146–47,_165_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBullough2004146–47,_165-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> If so, Alcuin's origins may lie in the southern part of what was formerly known as <a href="/wiki/Deira" title="Deira">Deira</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBullough2004165_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBullough2004165-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="York">York</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: York">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The young Alcuin came to the <a href="/wiki/York_Minster" title="York Minster">cathedral church</a> of York during the golden age of Archbishop <a href="/wiki/Ecgbert,_Archbishop_of_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Ecgbert, Archbishop of York">Ecgbert</a> and his brother, the Northumbrian King <a href="/wiki/Eadberht_of_Northumbria" title="Eadberht of Northumbria">Eadberht</a>. Ecgbert had been a disciple of the <a href="/wiki/Bede" title="Bede">Venerable Bede</a>, who urged him to raise York to an <a href="/wiki/Archbishopric" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishopric">archbishopric</a>. King Eadberht and Archbishop Ecgbert oversaw the re-energising and reorganisation of the <a href="/wiki/History_of_the_Church_of_England" title="History of the Church of England">English church</a>, with an emphasis on reforming the clergy and on the tradition of learning that Bede had begun. Ecgbert was devoted to Alcuin, who thrived under his tutelage.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The York school was renowned as a centre of learning in the liberal arts, literature, and science, as well as in religious matters.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHutchison2006_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHutchison2006-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> From here, Alcuin drew inspiration for the school he would lead at the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Frankish</a> court. He revived the school with the <a href="/wiki/Trivium_(education)" class="mw-redirect" title="Trivium (education)">trivium</a> and <a href="/wiki/Quadrivium" title="Quadrivium">quadrivium</a> disciplines,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurns1907_15-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns1907-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> writing a <a href="/wiki/Codex" title="Codex">codex</a> on the trivium, while his student <a href="/wiki/Rabanus_Maurus" title="Rabanus Maurus">Hraban</a> wrote one on the quadrivium.
</p><p>Alcuin graduated to become a teacher during the 750s. His ascendancy to the headship of the York school, the ancestor of <a href="/wiki/St_Peter%27s_School,_York" title="St Peter&#39;s School, York">St Peter's School</a>, began after <a href="/wiki/Ethelbert_of_York" class="mw-redirect" title="Ethelbert of York">Aelbert</a> became Archbishop of York in 767. Around the same time, Alcuin became a <a href="/wiki/Deacon" title="Deacon">deacon</a> in the church. He was never ordained a priest. Though no real evidence shows that he took monastic vows, he lived as if he had.
</p><p>In 781, King <a href="/wiki/%C3%86lfwald_I_of_Northumbria" title="Ælfwald I of Northumbria">Elfwald</a> sent Alcuin to Rome to petition the pope for official confirmation of York's status as an archbishopric and to confirm the election of the new archbishop, <a href="/wiki/Eanbald_I" class="mw-redirect" title="Eanbald I">Eanbald I</a>. On his way home, he met Charlemagne (whom he had met once before), this time in the Italian city of <a href="/wiki/Parma" title="Parma">Parma</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Charlemagne">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<ul><li><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/10px-Socrates.png" decoding="async" width="10" height="16" class="noviewer" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/15px-Socrates.png 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/cd/Socrates.png/21px-Socrates.png 2x" data-file-width="326" data-file-height="500" />&#160;<a href="/wiki/Portal:Philosophy" title="Portal:Philosophy">Philosophy&#32;portal</a></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/File:046CupolaSPietro.jpg" class="image"><img alt="icon" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/5a/046CupolaSPietro.jpg/16px-046CupolaSPietro.jpg" decoding="async" class="mw-redirect" title="Portal:Catholicism">Catholicism&#32;portal</a></li></ul></td></tr><tr><td class="sidebar-navbar"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1129693374"/><style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::before{margin-right:-0.125em;content:"[ "}.mw-parser-output .navbar-brackets::after{margin-left:-0.125em;content:" ]"}.mw-parser-output .navbar li{word-spacing:-0.125em}.mw-parser-output .navbar a>span,.mw-parser-output .navbar a>abbr{text-decoration:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-mini abbr{font-variant:small-caps;border-bottom:none;text-decoration:none;cursor:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-full{font-size:114%;margin:0 7em}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Scholasticism" title="Template:Scholasticism"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Scholasticism" title="Template talk:Scholasticism"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Scholasticism&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Alcuin's intellectual curiosity allowed him to be reluctantly persuaded to join Charlemagne's court. He joined an illustrious group of scholars whom Charlemagne had gathered around him, the mainsprings of the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance" title="Carolingian Renaissance">Carolingian Renaissance</a>: <a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Pisa" title="Peter of Pisa">Peter of Pisa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Saint_Paulinus_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Paulinus II">Paulinus of Aquileia</a>, Rado, and <a href="/wiki/Saint_Fulrad" class="mw-redirect" title="Saint Fulrad">Abbot Fulrad</a>. Alcuin would later write, "the Lord was calling me to the service of King Charles".
</p><p>Alcuin became master of the <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne%27s_Palace_in_Aachen" class="mw-redirect" title="Charlemagne&#39;s Palace in Aachen">Palace</a> School of Charlemagne in <a href="/wiki/Aachen" title="Aachen">Aachen</a> (<span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">Urbs Regale</i></span>) in 782.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurns1907_15-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns1907-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> It had been founded by the king's ancestors as a place for the education of the royal children (mostly in manners and the ways of the court). However, Charlemagne wanted to include the <a href="/wiki/Liberal_arts" class="mw-redirect" title="Liberal arts">liberal arts</a>, and most importantly, the study of religion. From 782 to 790, Alcuin taught Charlemagne himself, his sons <a href="/wiki/Pepin_the_Hunchback" title="Pepin the Hunchback">Pepin</a> and <a href="/wiki/Louis_the_Pious" title="Louis the Pious">Louis</a>, as well as young men sent to be educated at court, and the young clerics attached to the <a href="/wiki/Palatine_Chapel_in_Aachen" class="mw-redirect" title="Palatine Chapel in Aachen">palace chapel</a>. Bringing with him from York his assistants Pyttel, Sigewulf, and Joseph, Alcuin revolutionised the educational standards of the Palace School, introducing Charlemagne to the liberal arts and creating a personalised atmosphere of scholarship and learning, to the extent that the institution came to be known as the 'school of Master Albinus'.
</p><p>In this role as adviser, he took issue with the emperor's policy of forcing pagans to be baptised on pain of death, arguing, "Faith is a free act of the will, not a forced act. We must appeal to the conscience, not compel it by violence. You can force people to be baptised, but you cannot force them to believe." His arguments seem to have prevailed – Charlemagne abolished the death penalty for paganism in 797.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTENeedham200052_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTENeedham200052-17">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Charlemagne gathered the best men of every land in his court, and became far more than just the king at the centre. It seems that he made many of these men his closest friends and counsellors. They referred to him as 'David', a reference to the Biblical king <a href="/wiki/David_(biblical_king)" class="mw-redirect" title="David (biblical king)">David</a>. Alcuin soon found himself on intimate terms with Charlemagne and the other men at court, where pupils and masters were known by affectionate and jesting nicknames.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWilmot-Buxton192293_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWilmot-Buxton192293-18">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup> Alcuin himself was known as 'Albinus' or 'Flaccus'. While at <a href="/wiki/Aachen" title="Aachen">Aachen</a>, Alcuin bestowed pet names upon his pupils – derived mainly from <a href="/wiki/Virgil" title="Virgil">Virgil</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Eclogues" title="Eclogues">Eclogues</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJaeger199938_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJaeger199938-19">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> According to the <i>Encyclopædia Britannica</i>, "He loved Charlemagne and enjoyed the king's esteem, but his letters reveal that his fear of him was as great as his love."<sup id="cite_ref-EB_20-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Return_to_Northumbria_and_back_to_Francia">Return to Northumbria and back to Francia</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Return to Northumbria and back to Francia">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In 790, Alcuin returned from the court of Charlemagne to England, to which he had remained attached. He dwelt there for some time, but Charlemagne then invited him back to help in the fight against the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Adoptionism" title="Spanish Adoptionism">Adoptionist</a> heresy, which was at that time making great progress in <a href="/wiki/Toledo,_Spain" title="Toledo, Spain">Toledo</a>, the old capital of the <a href="/wiki/Visigoths" title="Visigoths">Visigoths</a> and still a major city for the Christians under <a href="/wiki/Al-Andalus" title="Al-Andalus">Islamic rule in Spain</a>. He is believed to have had contacts with <a href="/wiki/Beatus_of_Li%C3%A9bana" title="Beatus of Liébana">Beatus of Liébana</a>, from the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Asturias" title="Kingdom of Asturias">Kingdom of Asturias</a>, who fought against Adoptionism. At the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Frankfurt" title="Council of Frankfurt">Council of Frankfurt</a> in 794, Alcuin upheld the orthodox doctrine against the views expressed by <a href="/wiki/Felix,_Bishop_of_Urgel" class="mw-redirect" title="Felix, Bishop of Urgel">Felix of Urgel</a>, an <a href="/wiki/Heresiarch" title="Heresiarch">heresiarch</a> according to the Catholic Encyclopaedia.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBurns1907_15-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBurns1907-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Having failed during his stay in Northumbria to influence King <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelred_I_of_Northumbria" title="Æthelred I of Northumbria">Æthelred</a> in the conduct of his reign, Alcuin never returned home.
</p><p>He was back at Charlemagne's court by at least mid-792, writing a series of letters to Æthelred, to Hygbald, Bishop of <a href="/wiki/Lindisfarne" title="Lindisfarne">Lindisfarne</a>, and to <a href="/wiki/%C3%86thelhard" title="Æthelhard">Æthelhard</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Canterbury" title="Archbishop of Canterbury">Archbishop of Canterbury</a> in the succeeding months, dealing with the <a href="/wiki/Viking" class="mw-redirect" title="Viking">Viking</a> attack on Lindisfarne in July 793. These letters and Alcuin's poem on the subject, <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">"De clade Lindisfarnensis monasterii"</i></span>, provide the only significant contemporary account of these events. In his description of the Viking attack, he wrote: "Never before has such terror appeared in Britain. Behold the church of <a href="/wiki/St_Cuthbert" class="mw-redirect" title="St Cuthbert">St Cuthbert</a>, splattered with the blood of God's priests, robbed of its ornaments."
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Tours_and_death">Tours and death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Tours and death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1066479718"/><table class="infobox vcard"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above n" style="background-color:gold;"><div class="honorific-prefix" style="display:inline;font-size: 77%; font-weight: normal;"><a href="/wiki/Saint" title="Saint">Saint</a></div><br /><div style="display:inline;" class="fn">Alcuin of York</div></th></tr><tr><td colspan="2" class="infobox-image"><a href="/wiki/File:Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg" class="image"><img alt="Raban-Maur Alcuin Otgar.jpg" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg/220px-Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="234" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg/330px-Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/3/3a/Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg/440px-Raban-Maur_Alcuin_Otgar.jpg 2x" data-file-width="4679" data-file-height="4984" /></a></td></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header" style="background-color:gold;">Deacon, Scholar, Abbot of Tours</th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label">Venerated&#160;in</th><td class="infobox-data"><a href="/wiki/Anglican_Communion" title="Anglican Communion">Anglican Communion</a>, <a href="/wiki/Roman_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Roman Catholic Church">Roman Catholic Church</a>, as a blessed</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints" title="Calendar of saints">Feast</a></th><td class="infobox-data">20 May</td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>In 796, Alcuin was in his 60s. He hoped to be free from court duties and upon the death of Abbot Itherius of Saint Martin at Tours, Charlemagne put <a href="/wiki/Marmoutier_Abbey,_Tours" title="Marmoutier Abbey, Tours">Marmoutier Abbey</a> into Alcuin's care, with the understanding that he should be available if the king ever needed his counsel. There, he encouraged the work of the monks on the beautiful Carolingian minuscule script, ancestor of modern Roman typefaces.<sup id="cite_ref-EB_20-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-EB-20">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alcuin died on 19 May 804, some 10 years before the emperor, and was buried at St. Martin's Church under an epitaph that partly read:<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDuckett1951305_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDuckett1951305-21">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><div class="poem">
<p>Dust, worms, and ashes now&#160;...<br />
Alcuin my name, wisdom I always loved,<br />
Pray, reader, for my soul.
</p>
</div></blockquote>
<p>The majority of details on Alcuin's life come from his letters and poems. Also, autobiographical sections are in Alcuin's poem on York and in the <i>Vita Alcuini</i>, a <a href="/wiki/Hagiography" title="Hagiography">hagiography</a> written for him at Ferrières in the 820s, possibly based in part on the memories of Sigwulf, one of Alcuin's pupils.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Carolingian_Renaissance_figure_and_legacy">Carolingian Renaissance figure and legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Carolingian Renaissance figure and legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Mathematician">Mathematician</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Mathematician">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Propositiones_ad_acuendos_juvenes" class="mw-redirect" title="Propositiones ad acuendos juvenes">Propositiones ad acuendos juvenes</a></div>
<p>The collection of mathematical and logical word problems entitled <i>Propositiones ad acuendos juvenes</i> ("Problems to Sharpen Youths")<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAlcuinn.d._22-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAlcuinn.d.-22">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> is sometimes attributed to Alcuin.<sup id="cite_ref-Peterson-2005_23-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Peterson-2005-23">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEAtkinson2005354–62_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEAtkinson2005354–62-24">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> In a 799 letter to Charlemagne, the scholar claimed to have sent "certain figures of arithmetic for the joy of cleverness",<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> which some scholars have identified with the <i>Propositiones.</i><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEJullienPerelman1994482–83_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEJullienPerelman1994482–83-26">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
The text contains about 53 mathematical word problems (with solutions), in no particular pedagogical order. Among the most famous of these problems are: four that involve <a href="/wiki/River_crossing_problem" class="mw-redirect" title="River crossing problem">river crossings</a>, including the <a href="/wiki/Jealous_husbands_problem" class="mw-redirect" title="Jealous husbands problem">problem of three anxious brothers</a>, each of whom has an unmarried sister whom he cannot leave alone with either of the other men lest she be defiled<sup id="cite_ref-3-brothers_28-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-3-brothers-28">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> (Problem 17); the <a href="/wiki/Fox,_goose_and_bag_of_beans_puzzle" class="mw-redirect" title="Fox, goose and bag of beans puzzle">problem of the wolf, goat, and cabbage</a> (Problem 18); and the problem of "the two adults and two children where the children weigh half as much as the adults" (Problem 19). <a href="/wiki/Alcuin%27s_sequence" title="Alcuin&#39;s sequence">Alcuin's sequence</a> is the solution to one of the problems of that book.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Literary_influence">Literary influence</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Literary influence">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Alcuin made the abbey school into a model of excellence and many students flocked to it. He had many manuscripts copied using outstandingly beautiful <a href="/wiki/Calligraphy" title="Calligraphy">calligraphy</a>, the Carolingian minuscule based on round and legible <a href="/wiki/Uncial" class="mw-redirect" title="Uncial">uncial</a> letters. He wrote many letters to his English friends, to <a href="/wiki/Arno_of_Salzburg" title="Arno of Salzburg">Arno, bishop of Salzburg</a> and above all to Charlemagne. These letters (of which 311 are extant) are filled mainly with pious meditations, but they form an important source of information as to the literary and social conditions of the time and are the most reliable authority for the history of <a href="/wiki/Humanism" title="Humanism">humanism</a> during the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian" class="mw-redirect" title="Carolingian">Carolingian</a> age. Alcuin trained the numerous monks of the abbey in piety, and in the midst of these pursuits, he died.
</p><p>Alcuin is the most prominent figure of the <a href="/wiki/Carolingian_Renaissance" title="Carolingian Renaissance">Carolingian Renaissance</a>, in which three main periods have been distinguished: in the first of these, up to the arrival of Alcuin at the court, the Italians occupy a central place; in the second, Alcuin and the English are dominant; in the third (from 804), the influence of <a href="/wiki/Theodulf_of_Orl%C3%A9ans" title="Theodulf of Orléans">Theodulf the Visigoth</a> is preponderant.
</p><p>Alcuin also developed manuals used in his educational work – a <a href="/wiki/Grammar" title="Grammar">grammar</a> and works on <a href="/wiki/Rhetoric" title="Rhetoric">rhetoric</a> and <a href="/wiki/Dialectics" class="mw-redirect" title="Dialectics">dialectics</a>. These are written in the form of a <a href="/wiki/Dialogue" title="Dialogue">dialogue</a>, and in two of them the interlocutors are Charlemagne and Alcuin. He wrote several <a href="/wiki/Theological" class="mw-redirect" title="Theological">theological</a> treatises: a <i>De fide Trinitatis</i>, and commentaries on the Bible.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEPage190915_29-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEPage190915-29">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup> Alcuin is credited with inventing the first known <a href="/wiki/Question_mark" title="Question mark">question mark</a>, though it did not resemble the modern symbol.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTETruss200376_30-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTETruss200376-30">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alcuin transmitted to the <a href="/wiki/Franks" title="Franks">Franks</a> the knowledge of Latin culture, which had existed in Anglo-Saxon England. A number of his works still exist. Besides some graceful epistles in the style of <a href="/wiki/Venantius_Fortunatus" title="Venantius Fortunatus">Venantius Fortunatus</a>, he wrote some long poems, and notably he is the author of a history (in verse) of the church at York, <i>Versus de patribus, regibus et sanctis Eboracensis ecclesiae</i>. At the same time, he is noted for making one of the only explicit comments on <a href="/wiki/Old_English_poetry" class="mw-redirect" title="Old English poetry">Old English poetry</a> surviving from the early Middle Ages, in a letter to one Speratus, the bishop of an unnamed English <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_see" title="Episcopal see">see</a> (possibly <a href="/wiki/Unwona" title="Unwona">Unwona</a> of Leicester): <span title="Latin-language text"><i lang="la">"verba Dei legantur in sacerdotali convivio: ibi decet lectorem audiri, non citharistam; sermones patrum, non carmina gentilium. Quid <a href="/wiki/Hinieldus" class="mw-redirect" title="Hinieldus">Hinieldus</a> cum Christo?"</i></span> ("Let God's words be read at the episcopal dinner-table. It is right that a reader should be heard, not a harpist, patristic discourse, not pagan song. What has <a href="/wiki/Ingeld" title="Ingeld">Ingeld</a> to do with Christ?").<sup id="cite_ref-31" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-31">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Use_of_homoerotic_language_in_writings">Use of homoerotic language in writings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Use of homoerotic language in writings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Historian <a href="/wiki/John_Boswell" title="John Boswell">John Boswell</a> cited Alcuin's writings as demonstrating a personal outpouring of his internalized <a href="/wiki/Homosexual" class="mw-redirect" title="Homosexual">homosexual</a> feelings.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBoswell2015189_32-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBoswell2015189-32">&#91;30&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBromell200216_33-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBromell200216-33">&#91;31&#93;</a></sup> Others agree that Alcuin at times "comes perilously close to communicating openly his same-sex desires", and this reflects the erotic subculture of the Carolingian monastic school, but also perhaps a 'queer space' where "erotic attachment and affections may be safely articulated".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECoon201118_34-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECoon201118-34">&#91;32&#93;</a></sup> According to David Clark, passages in some of Alcuin's writings can be seen to display homosocial desire, even possibly homoerotic imagery. However, he argues that it is not possible to necessarily determine whether they were the result of an outward expression of erotic feelings on the part of Alcuin.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClark200980_35-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClark200980-35">&#91;33&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The interpretation of homosexual desire has been disputed by <a href="/wiki/Allen_Frantzen" title="Allen Frantzen">Allen Frantzen</a>, who identifies Alcuin's language with that of medieval Christian <i>amicitia</i> or friendship.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEFrantzen1998198_36-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEFrantzen1998198-36">&#91;34&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-37" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-37">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> Douglas Dales and <a href="/wiki/Rowan_Williams" title="Rowan Williams">Rowan Williams</a> say "the use of language drawn [by Alcuin] from the <i>Song of Songs</i> transforms apparently erotic language into something within Christian friendship – 'an ordained affection<span style="padding-right:.15em;">'</span>".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDalesWilliams2013228_38-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDalesWilliams2013228-38">&#91;35&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Alcuin was also a close friend of Charlemagne's sister <a href="/wiki/Gisela,_Abbess_of_Chelles" title="Gisela, Abbess of Chelles">Gisela, Abbess of Chelles</a>, and he hailed her as "a noble sister in the bond of sweet love".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDales201290_39-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDales201290-39">&#91;36&#93;</a></sup> He wrote to Charlemagne's daughters Rotrudis and Bertha, "the devotion of my heart specially tends towards you both because of the familiarity and dedication you have shown me".<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDales201291_40-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDales201291-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup> He dedicated the last two books of his commentary on John's gospel to them both.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEDales201291_40-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEDales201291-40">&#91;37&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Despite inconclusive evidence of Alcuin's personal passions, he was clear in his own writings that the men of <a href="/wiki/Sodom_and_Gomorrah" title="Sodom and Gomorrah">Sodom</a> had been punished with fire for "sinning against nature with men"&#160;– a view commonly held by the Church at the time. Such sins, argued Alcuin, were therefore more serious than lustful acts with women, for which the earth was cleansed and revivified by the water of the <a href="/wiki/Genesis_flood_narrative" title="Genesis flood narrative">Flood</a>, and merit to be "withered by flames unto eternal barrenness".<sup id="cite_ref-41" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-41">&#91;38&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Legacy">Legacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Legacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Alcuin is honored in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">Church of England</a> and in the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Episcopal_Church)" title="Calendar of saints (Episcopal Church)">Episcopal Church</a> on 20 May the first available day after the day of his death (as <a href="/wiki/Dunstan" title="Dunstan">Dunstan</a> is celebrated on 19 May).<sup id="cite_ref-42" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-42">&#91;39&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-43" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-43">&#91;40&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Alcuin_College" class="mw-redirect" title="Alcuin College">Alcuin College</a>, one of the <a href="/wiki/Colleges_of_the_University_of_York" title="Colleges of the University of York">colleges of the University of York</a>, is named after him.<sup id="cite_ref-44" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-44">&#91;41&#93;</a></sup> In January 2020, Alcuin was the subject of the <a href="/wiki/BBC_Radio_4" title="BBC Radio 4">BBC Radio 4</a> programme <i><a href="/wiki/In_Our_Time_(radio_series)" title="In Our Time (radio series)">In Our Time</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-45" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-45">&#91;42&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Selected_works">Selected works</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Selected works">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>For a complete census of Alcuin's works, see Marie-Hélène Jullien and Françoise Perelman, eds., <i>Clavis scriptorum latinorum medii aevi: Auctores Galliae 735–987. Tomus II: Alcuinus.</i> Turnhout: Brepols, 1999.
</p>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Letters">Letters</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=14" title="Edit section: Letters">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Theology">Theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=16" title="Edit section: Theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Hagiography">Hagiography</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=17" title="Edit section: Hagiography">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><i>Vita II Vedastis episcopi Atrebatensis</i>. Revision of the earlier <i>Vita Vedastis</i> by <a href="/wiki/Jonas_of_Bobbio" title="Jonas of Bobbio">Jonas of Bobbio</a>. <i>Patrologia Latina</i> 101: 663–682.</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Richarius" title="Richarius">Vita Richarii confessoris Centulensis</a></i>. Revision of an earlier anonymous life. MGH Scriptores Rerum Merovingicarum 4: 381–401.</li>

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Alcuin&amp;action=edit&amp;section=20" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-16"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-16">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFMayr-Harting2016">Mayr-Harting 2016</a>, p.&#160;207 asserts Charlemagne met Alcuin – for the second time – at Parma in 781. <a href="#CITEREFStory2005">Story 2005</a>, p.&#160;137 reports that Alcuin had previously been sent to Charlemagne by Ethelbert.</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-27"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-27">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">A more skeptical attitude toward Alcuin's authorship of this text and others is taken by <a href="#CITEREFGorman2002">Gorman 2002</a>, pp.&#160;101–30 </span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-37"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-37">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">See also <a href="#CITEREFJaeger1991">Jaeger 1991</a> </span>
